It is named Kadambavanam because this place was formerly a forest filled with Kadamba trees. Later, at the command of the Lord, Kulasekara Pandiyan cleared the forest and made it a country. As a symbol of its past as Kadamba Vanam, the Kadamba tree is the Sthala Vriksham (sacred tree) of this place. Hence, it is called Kadambavanam. It has another name, Aalavaai. To indicate the boundary of Madurai city to the king, the Lord took His serpent bracelet and let it go. It coiled in a circle, biting its own tail. That circular shape was taken as the boundary. Since the serpent showed the boundary by biting its tail, this place is called Aalavaai and Thiru Aalavaai. It has another name, Madurai. The Lord sanctified this place by sprinkling the nectar (madhu) from the crescent moon on His matted hair, hence it is called Madurai. It has another name, Naanmadakkoodal. To stop the seven clouds sent by Varuna to destroy Madurai, Lord Shiva released four clouds from His matted hair. These clouds rose like mountains and gathered as four mansions, protecting the city, hence it is called Naanmadakkoodal. This is Jivan Muktipuram. It is named so because it grants Jivan Mukti to those who reside here. It is also called Kannipuram, Kanni Puri, Kannika Kshetram, and Kanni Nadu. This is because the all-powerful Parasakti, as Thadathagai Pirattiyar, ruled here as a maiden. It is named Samashti Vichapuram. It is called Samashti Vichapuram because it bestows all kinds of knowledge, including Shiva Jnanam. Samashti Vichapuram means the complete city of Shiva Jnanam. It is named so because it bestows the four paths of Charya, Kriya, Yoga, and Jnana upon souls. Furthermore, in the form of Aruvuruv Linga, there are two types: Vyashti Lingam and Samashti Lingam. Among these, the Sokkanathar Peruman, the Sokkalinga idol here, is a Samashti Lingam, hence this place is called Samashti Vichapuram. It is named Dwadasanta Thalam. This place is called Dwadasanta Thalam because it is located 12 inches above the head of the Virat Purusha. Since it is a Niradhara Paravali Thalam beyond Adhara, it is also called Dwadasantapuram and Dwadasanta Thalam. It has another name, Sarva Theerthapuram. Goddess Uma established all 136 sacred waters of Thirukailayam as sacred waters here. Hence, it is called Sarva Theerthapuram. Besides these, since Lord Shiva Himself ruled as Sundara Pandiyan, it is also called Shivarajadhani, Shivanagaram, and Bhuloka Kailasam. This is the place where Sangam poets resided and researched Tamil. Therefore, Madurai also has names like Tamil Madurai, Tamil Koodal, Sangam Tamil Koodal, Muchchangam Valarkkoodal, Viri Tamil Koodal, Muthamizh Madurai. The sacred water of this place is Potramarai Theertham. This Potramarai Theertham is a sacred water created by Lord Somasundarar for His own ablutions even before rivers like Ganga and Kaveri, which are like kings among rivers, and oceans and clouds and other water bodies came into existence. Since it appeared before all other sacred waters, it is called Adhi Theertham. Since it shines above all other sacred waters, it is called Parama Theertham. Since it bestowed auspiciousness upon those who bathed in it, it is called Shiva Theertham. Since it bestowed knowledge, it is called Jnana Theertham, and since it bestows liberation, it is called Mukti Theertham. Lord Shiva Himself says that those who bathe in this sacred water, perform tarpana, give charity, chant the sacred five letters from its bank, lovingly approach, worship, circumambulate, and praise Him, will attain the benefit of having received human birth. Furthermore, the Lord Himself says that if we perform ablutions with this sacred water, we will attain the benefit of having performed ablutions with all the exalted sacred waters in all 14 worlds. Merely beholding the Potramarai Theertham, located inside the Meenakshi Amman Temple in Madurai, removes sins. Bathing in it fulfills all desired objects. The blessings attained by those who perform Jnana Snanam, Agneya Snanam, Mantra Snanam, Mrittika Snanam, Divya Snanam, Kapila Snanam, Vibhuti Snanam, Manasa Snanam, Vayu Snanam, Nirmalya Snanam, and other such purifying baths, are not comparable to the blessings attained by bathing in the Potramarai Theertham. Those who bathe in this Potramarai Theertham and worship Lord Somasundarar attain supreme liberation. Those who bathe in it without faith attain heaven. This is the glory of the sacred water of Madurai. First, we saw the glory of the place. Then we saw the glory of the sacred water. Now let us contemplate the glory of the Murthi, the Moolalinga Murthi. The glory of Lord Somasundarar, the Moolalinga Murthi, who resides and bestows grace in Thiru Aalavaai, also known as Madurai, is immeasurable. The Shiva Lingam appeared here under the Kadamba tree even before Shiva Linga Murthis appeared in places like Mount Meru, Thirukailayam, Mandara Mountain, Thirukkedaram, and Kasi. Only after this did all the Shiva Lingams in other places appear. Therefore, this Shiva Lingam, which is the form of the light of knowledge, is called Moolalingam. This can be understood from the Thevaram lines of Appar Peruman, "Mulaiththane Ellaarkkum Munne Thondri." The lines from Thiru Aalavaaiyudaiyar Thiruvilaiyadal Puranam, "Medhini Thondrum Munnan Sevviyan Mulaiththezhundha Divya Lingam," also convey the same. This Linga Thirumeni, endowed with such greatness, is a Shiva Linga Thirumeni that possesses the beautiful form of Jnana, with 32 auspicious marks, and always bestows grace with Goddess Uma. All the Devas named Him Somasundara Kadavul. Somasundara Lingam means a beautiful Shiva Lingam accompanied by Goddess Uma. This Somasundara Peruman pervaded the entire world. That is why when the Pandya king struck Him on the back with the golden cane in his hand, that mark appeared on all animate and inanimate objects in all three worlds. Those who have had the darshan of this Samashti form of Somasundara Kadavul have had the darshan of all the Vyashti forms of Shiva Linga Murthis in other sacred places. Lord Shiva, who is without beginning or end, who stands as the all-pervading fullness, and who bestows bondage and liberation upon souls, always remains manifest in this Sokkalingam, praised by the Vedas, in order to bestow the three – this world, the next world, and liberation – upon those who worship Him. Those who have had the darshan of this supreme Murthi are those who have had the darshan of the supreme light of Shiva that appears within when speech and mind subside. Here, the pure souls who regularly worship Lord Somasundarar with love four times a day are freed from the karmas of their previous births, attain purity, receive divine wealth and other riches, enjoy worldly pleasures, and then, freed from the impurity of anava, attain liberation. Hearing the sacred name of Somasundara Kadavul removes the karmas of ten births. Thinking of Him removes the great sins of a hundred births. If one rises and steps out of the house saying, "Let us now go and worship Lord Sokkalinga," the heinous sins of a thousand births are removed. Merely having the darshan of Somasundara Kadavul yields the benefit of having performed a thousand Ashwamedha Yagas. Those who praise and worship Him with Rudra Sukta mantras attain the benefit of having performed a thousand Rajasuyam Yagas. Offering one flower on the sacred head of Somasundara Kadavul yields the benefit of having worshipped with a hundred golden flowers in other places. Those who offer incense to Somasundara Kadavul once are freed from a thousand faults committed by mind, speech, and body. Those who offer sacred food (Thiru Amudu) to the Lord will enjoy a yuga of Shiva Loka life for each grain of rice in the offering. Those who offer betel leaves will live in Shiva Puram for a hundred crore divine years. Those who light camphor lamps attain Shiva Sarupyam (the form of Shiva). Those who worship Lord Sokkalinga once, either with Ashtanga (eight-limbed) or Panchanga (five-limbed) prostration, will live as kings wielding the scepter. Homam, penance, japa, charity, etc., performed in the presence of such a glorious Somasundara Kadavul, even if atomic in size, will be considered as vast as Mount Meru. Who can measure and describe all the glories of this Shiva Linga Murthi, who possesses such immense greatness? Sage Agastya is quoted in Thiruvilaiyadal Puranam as saying, "O sages, we have spoken a little of what we know." Thus, we have contemplated the glory of Thiru Aalavaai, the glory of Potramarai Theertham, and the glory of Lord Somasundarar, the Murthi. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 04 — Murthi Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 183, Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Murthi Nayanar Puranam. We are continuing to contemplate the Sthala Purana of Madurai, the sacred city where Murthi Nayanar was born. Lord Somasundarar, enshrined here, has many sacred names such as Karpoora Sundarar, Kadambavana Sundarar, Kalyana Sundarar, Abhirama Sundarar, Shanbhaga Sundarar, Maguda Sundarar, Kasturi Sundarar, Pazhiyanchiya Sundarar, Somasundarar, Aalavaai Sundarar, Naanmada Koodal Nayagar, Madhurapathi Vendhar, Samashti Vichapura Nadhar, Jivan Mukthipura Nadhar, Bhuloka Shivalokadhipar, Kannipuresar, Moolalinga Murthi. All these glories of the Sthala that we have seen are said to have been taught by Lord Muruga to Sage Agastya in the Thiruvilaiyadal Puranam. In inscriptions, the Lord is referred to by names such as Azhagiya Sokkanathar, Thiru Thiru Aalavaaiyudaiya Nayanar, Madurai Udaiyar, Athira Veesi Vilaiyaaduvar, Sokkanatha Eswara Udaiyar, Mahadevar Azhagiya Sokkanar, Sundara Pandiya Chozha Konar, Thiru Aalavaaiyudaiya Thambiranar. Here, the Goddess's name is Meenakshi, and in Tamil, Angayarkanni. The Goddess is also known by various names such as Thadathagai Piratti, Abhisheka Valli, Karpoora Valli, Maragatha Valli, Sundara Valli, Abhirama Valli, Kayarkan Kumari, Kumarithuraiyaval, Komagal, Pandiya Piratti, Manikka Valli, Madhurapuri Thalaivi, Mudumalai Thiru Vazhudhigal. In inscriptions, she is referred to as Thirukkamakkottathu Aaludaiya Naachiyar. This Sthalam is known as Velliambalam (Silver Hall) among the Pancha Sabhas (five halls) where the Lord performs His dance. The 64 Thiruvilaiyadals (divine sports) performed by the Lord here can be seen in detail in the Thiruvilaiyadal Puranam. There is also a temple here where Lord Somasundarar Himself, to make the world understand the glory of Shiva Puja and attain salvation, consecrated Himself as an Atmaartha Murthi and worshipped, known as "Immaiye Nanmai Tharuvar" (He who bestows good in this life). Other sacred waters in this place include Vaigai Theertham, Ezhu Kadal Theertham, Krithamalai Theertham, Kondali Theertham, and Theppakulam. The Madurai temple offers a unique opportunity to see together hundred-pillared halls, thousand-pillared halls, etc., which are not found in any other texts except Saiva Agamas, as well as the 25 forms of deities found in Shiva Agamas, their meditative forms, the five types of Nritta Murthis (dancing forms) and their postures, and divine images made in five materials: gold, silver, copper, stone, and stucco. Furthermore, one can observe the various types of Upapeetha structures like Padabandham, Padmabandham, Pratyantaram found in Saiva Agamas and Shilpa Shastras, and the diverse and artistic variations of Vimanas like Kailasam, Meru, Mandaram. Thus, the Madurai temple stands as an art gallery for the world. The Ganesha of this place is Siddhi Vinayagar. Murugan is Koodal Kumarar. The Vimana is Indra Vimana. Now let's look at the temple structure. The city of Madurai itself is shaped like a lotus flower. In its center is the Meenakshi Sundareswarar Temple, surrounded by beautifully laid out streets. Here, the Lord's Vimana is called Indra Vimana. This Indra Vimana was built and worshipped by Indra to be freed from his curse. The Madurai temple is located in the center of the city, facing east. There are four large Rajagopurams (temple towers) on all four sides of the temple. First, the East Gopuram. This is located directly opposite the Swami Sannidhi, in the eastern entrance area adjacent to the East Aadi Veedhi. Among the four gopurams, this is the oldest. It also has another name, Sundara Pandiyan Gopuram. Next, the South Gopuram. This is the tallest among the four gopurams. It has nine tiers and contains 1511 stucco sculptures depicting mythological stories. Next, the West Gopuram. Its height is 154 feet. It has 1124 stucco sculptures. Next, the North Gopuram. This also has nine tiers and is 152 feet high. Compared to the other gopurams, it has the fewest stucco sculptures, only 404. Besides these, there is a gopuram called Raya Gopuram. The gopurams of the Swami temple include Sannidhi Gopuram, Nayak Gopuram, Nadukkattu Gopuram, Palagai Gopuram, and Idabakkuri Gopuram. The Vimana of the Swami's sanctum sanctorum was built by Indra. Eight Dikgajas (elephants of the cardinal directions), two on each side, are depicted supporting this Vimana on all four sides of the sanctum sanctorum. Next are the Amman temple gopurams: Chithira Gopuram, Sannidhi Gopuram, Kadaga Gopuram, and the sanctum sanctorum Vimana. The Meenakshi Sundareswarar temple has three circumambulatory paths (prakarams). The first two circuits are inside the temple, separately for Sundareswarar and Meenakshi Amman. The third circumambulatory path is inside the compound wall. This is called Aadi Veedhi. The area outside the compound wall is called Chithirai Veedhi. The Amman temple has a direct entrance. Generally, it is customary in this temple to have darshan of Meenakshi Amman first, and then Lord Sundareswarar. We enter the temple through the Ashtashakti Mandapam in front of the Meenakshi Amman temple. It contains sculptures of eight Shaktis. On the south side are the forms of Kaumari, Raudri, Vaishnavi, and Mahalakshmi, and on the north side are the forms of Yagnaroopini, Shyamala, Maheswari, and Manonmani, each on separate pillars. The history of Sri Meenakshi Amman is depicted in paintings in this mandapam. To the right of the entrance next to this mandapam is Vallabhai Ganapathi, and to the left is Shanmugar. After worshipping them and entering, there are sculptures of a hunter on the north and a huntress on the south. Next to this is the Meenakshi Nayakkan Mandapam, built by Meenakshi Nayakkar, one of Thirumalai Nayakkar's ministers. On the ceiling of this mandapam, the 12 zodiac signs are arranged in a square shape. In the next gopuram entrance, large Thiruvasi lamps are lit daily. Passing this, one finds the Mudali Mandapam. In its northern row are sculptures of Bhikshadanar, Rishipattini, and Tharuka Munis, and in the southern row are sculptures of Munivar, Mohini, and Kadanthai Mudaliyar. Proceeding further inside, one sees the Potramarai Kulam (Golden Lotus Pond). This is the place where the Sangappalagai (Sangam board) appeared, which upheld the greatness of Thirukkural. This is also the place where Sri La Sri Gurugnanasambandar, the Adhi Gurumurthi of Dharmapuram Adheenam, received the Sokkalingam by the grace of Lord Shiva. To the west of the Potramarai Kulam, opposite the Sri Meenakshi Sannidhi, is the Kilikkattu Mandapam. It is also called Yali Mandapam or Sangili Mandapam. This mandapam houses the main entrance to the Meenakshi Amman Sannidhi. Here, the Siddhi Vinayagar Sannidhi, specific to this temple, is on the right, and the Kumarar Sannidhi is on the left. From here, there is the Nadukkattu Gopuram entrance leading to the Swami temple. The entrance to the Madappalli (kitchen) is located here. Near the Madappalli entrance is the flower-tying area. Next to it is the sandalwood grinding stone used by our Murthi Nayanar. From here, near the entrance to the Amman Sannidhi, are the Siddhi Vinayagar Sannidhi and Murugan's Sannidhi. One must go through the Vembathoorar Gopuram to enter the Amman temple from here. Entering through the Vembathoorar Gopuram from the Kilikkattu Mandapam leads to the second prakaram. In this prakaram are the Thirumalai Vinayagar Sannidhi, Thirumalai Subramaniar Sannidhi, Kodimaram (flagstaff), Thirumalai Nayakkar statue, Kolu Mandapam, Vinayagar Sannidhi, Amman temple West Gopuram, Koodalingar Sannidhi, Koodal Kumarar Sannidhi, and Aarukaal Peeda Mandapam. Directly opposite the Amman Sannidhi, the Kodimaram is located in this second prakaram. In most temples, the Balipitham (sacrificial altar) and Kodimaram are located in the same place. However, here the Kodikkambam is in the second prakaram, and the Balipitham is in the Kilikkootu Mandapam. At the entrance to the inner prakaram from this prakaram, there are two dwarapalika (female guardian) sculptures. Entering through the main hall of this second prakaram leads to the Mahamandapam. The first prakaram of the Amman temple begins and ends in this mandapam. To the west of the Mahamandapam are the Ardhamandapam and the sanctum sanctorum of Mother Meenakshi. In this prakaram, to the left of the Mahamandapam, there is a Vinayagar, and on the way to the sanctum sanctorum, there are two Vinayagar sculptures. In the southwest corner, there is a small double Vinayagar temple, and in the northwest corner, there is a Murugan temple. Furthermore, Chandikeswari is also enshrined in this prakaram. The Palliyarai (bedchamber) is also in this prakaram. The mandapam in front of the Moolasthanam (sanctum sanctorum) is the Ardhamandapam. This is where the priests performing puja chant Vedas and worship. From here, at the entrance to the Garbhagriham (sanctum sanctorum) of Meenakshi Amman, there are sculptures of Vinayagar and Murugan on both sides. There is the sanctum sanctorum mandapam. In this mandapam, during Deeparadhana (lamp offering) times, Odhuvar Murthis stand and sing Thevaram Thirumurai songs. Going further inside from here, there is the Moolasthanam, the Garbhagriham. There, the Moola Vigraham (main idol) of Meenakshi Ambika, the Moolasthana Murthi, is enshrined in an exquisite form, embodying compassion with a smiling face. The divine form of Meenakshi Amman, my mother, is seen here in a standing posture, holding a flower in her right hand and her left hand hanging down. A green parrot is perched on the Amman's right shoulder. The divine form of Ambika, made of green emerald stone, appears with a childlike face and eyes brimming with grace. The old Thiruvilaiyadal sings of the Amman's divine form: "Ponguru selvam kalvi porivilaa vaaimai thuymai ingu emakku arulavalla inaiyilaa empiraatti pangayaththadan sool koodal pavalamal varaiyai neengaa angayarkanni mangai adikkamalangal potri." Paranjothi Munivar's Thiruvilaiyadal Puranam praises Meenakshi Ambika as: "Surumbu mural kadimalar poonguzhal potri uththariya thodiththol potri karum puruvach chilai potri kavuniyarkku paal surandha kalasam potri irumbu manam kuzhaiththennai eduththaanda angayarkkan empiraatti arumbum ilanagai potri aarana noopuranjilambum adigal potri." As praised, we too praise and worship the Amman. Circumambulating her, in the Deva Koshtam, around the Amman's sanctum sanctorum, are enshrined the Ambikas of Iccha Shakti, Kriya Shakti, and Jnana Shakti. Then, Chandikeswari Devi is also enshrined. After having their darshan, to the right of the first prakaram is the Palliyarai (bedchamber). After this, coming out, one can reach the second prakaram of the Swami temple from the Amman temple's Kilikkootu Mandapam in the second circumambulatory path. In the southern part of this, near the Nadukkattu Gopuram, is the Mukkuruni Vinayagar temple, about eight feet tall. In the western part of the Swami temple's second prakaram, many Lingams are consecrated. In the northwest of this prakaram is the Sangappulavar temple. It contains the idol of Iraiyanar and the statues of 49 Sangam poets, starting with Nakkirar, the head of the Sangam. There are eight flagstaffs in this second prakaram alone. Apart from this second prakaram belonging to the Swami, this prakaram also contains the West Gopuram, Idabakkuri Gopuram, Mandapa Nayagam (a hundred-pillared mandapam), and the Gnanasambandar temple. Adjacent to the northern prakaram of the second prakaram is the Kalyana Sundareswarar temple. After that, the hundred-pillared mandapam is located in the northeast corner of the second prakaram. In the southeast corner is the Nalvar temple. In the eastern part of the second prakaram is the Kambathadi Mandapam. The pillars of this mandapam have sculptures of Lord Shiva's 25 forms, along with Agni Veerabhadrar, Aghora Veerabhadrar, Urdhva Thandavar, and Kali. From here, at the entrance to the Swami's first prakaram, on both sides are Anugnai Vinayagar, Pazhani Andavar, Thanigai Kumarar, and Swamimalai Kumarar. The Nandi Mandapam is located directly opposite the Swami Sannidhi, between the pillar sculptures of the Kambathadi Mandapam. In the center of this mandapam are the flagstaff and the Balipitham. Entering the first prakaram from the second prakaram, on the eastern side are a Lingam and the Sun with his consorts. Vandiyamai also appears. Passing them, if one comes to the southern side, one can see the 63 Nayanmars. Next to them are the divine forms of the Santhana Acharyas, Sahasralingam, Kalaivagal, and Saptamatar. In the southwest corner of the first prakaram is the Somasakandar Mandapam. It houses the Utsava Murthis (processional deities) of Meenakshi Sundareswarar. In its western row, the Utsava Murthi of Murugan, Shodasa Lingam, Murugan, Juradevar, Juralingam, Panchamukha Lingam, and Bhikshadanar are seen. In the right Arkka corner are the Kasi Viswanathar Sannidhi and Medha Dakshinamurthi Sannidhi. In the northern part of the first prakaram are the Ellaam Valla Siddhar temple and the Chandikeswarar temple. Near it is the Kadamba tree, the Sthala Vriksham of Madurai. Near this is the Kanaka Sabhai (Golden Hall). Next to this hall are the 51 Akshara Lingam and the Mahalakshmi Sannidhi. Next to Lakshmi are the Potpadiyan Sannidhi and Lord Shiva as Nataraja in dancing posture, with Sivakami Ambal nearby. This is called Rathina Sabhai (Jewel Hall). There is a small covered well here. Here are a stone Vanni tree and a Lingam. In the eastern part of the prakaram are the Sannidhis of Bhairavar, Bhuvalingam, and Chandra with his consorts Karthigai and Rohini Deviyar. From this first prakaram, we proceed towards the Swami's sanctum sanctorum. Here is the Aarukaal Peeda Mandapam. At its entrance, on both sides, are installed two dwarapalaka sculptures made of stucco. Next is the Mahamandapam. Here, the copper idols of the 63 Nayanmars are placed. Here, Velliambala Natarajar appears in a dancing posture with 10 arms. This is called Velliambalam among the five halls where Lord Shiva dances. Here, the Lord performs the Ananda Thandava (dance of bliss), unusually lifting His right leg and resting on His left leg. This uniqueness of the Lord dancing with a changed leg is exclusive to Madurai. Next is the Ardha Mandapam. This mandapam is the front hall connected to the Moolasthanam. Then comes the Moolasthanam. The Swami temple's Moolasthanam is situated with an Upapeetam. Lord Somasundarar, enshrined here as Sokkalinga Thirumeni, is the primeval Lingam, having appeared before all other Shiva Lingams in all other places. He shines as the Samashti Lingam. As Thiruvilaiyadal Puranam praises and glorifies Lord Sokkalinga: "Venrule pulangal aindhaar mey unar ullam thorum senrule amudham ootrum thiruvarul potri etru kunrule irundhu kaatchi koduththarul kolam potri manrule maazhi aadum maraichchilambadigal potri," we too worship Him. Coming out, in the Deva Koshtam, to the south is Dakshinamurthi, to the west is Lingodbhavar, and to the north is Durga. We can worship them and come out. Thus is the structure of the Meenakshi Sundareswarar temple. Madurai and this temple are places abundant in festivals. Madurai is foremost among the places where festivals occur in all 12 months of the year. Here, daily, the Thiruvandhal festival puja, Kaalasandhi, Thrikalasandhi, Uchchikalam, Saayaratchai, Ardha Jamam, and Palliyarai pujas are performed. Here, in the month of Chithirai, the Thirukalyana festival; in Vaigasi, the Vasanthotsavam; in Aani, the Oonjal Utsavam; in Aadi, the Mulaikkottu Utsavam; in Aavani, the Pittu festival; in Purattasi, the Navaratri festival; in Aippasi, the Kolattam festival and Pavithrotsavam; in Karthigai, the Deepa festival; in Margazhi, the Ennai Kappu festival, Ashtami Sapparam; in Thai, the Theppa festival; in Maasi, the Maasi Magam festival for one mandalam (48 days); and in Panguni, the Kodai Vasantha festival are celebrated. The number of saints in this place is immeasurable. The number of Tamil and Sanskrit texts singing about this place is also immeasurable. Thus, we have contemplated a little about the Sthala Purana and glories of Madurai. Contemplating the 64 Thiruvilaiyadals performed by Lord Somasundarar in Madurai, we conclude this Sthala Purana and will continue with the Purana of Murthi Nayanar. The 64 Thiruvilaiyadals performed by Lord Somasundarar in Madurai are: Curing Indra's curse, Curing the white elephant's curse, Discovering the sacred city, Incarnation of Thadathagai Pirattiyar, Marriage of Thadathagai Pirattiyar, Performing the sacred dance in Velliambalam, Feeding Kundodharan, Invoking the Annakkuzhi and Vaigai, Invoking the seven seas, Invoking Malayathuvajan, Incarnation of Ukkira Pandiyan, Giving spear, conch, and discus to Ukkira Pandiyan, Releasing the spear to dry up the sea, Casting net over Indra's crown, Striking Meru with a stick, Revealing the meaning of Vedas, Selling rubies, Drying up the sea released by Varuna, Becoming Naanmada Koodal, Becoming Ellaam Valla Siddhar, Giving sugarcane to a stone elephant, Shooting an elephant, Becoming an old young boy, Dancing with changed leg, Fearing sin, Removing great sin, Cutting off a limb, Shooting a serpent, Killing a magical cow, Showing the true form, Bestowing the ploughman's parrot, Selling bangles, Teaching Ashtama Siddhi, Imprinting the bull emblem, Setting up a water pandal, Performing alchemy, Defeating the Chola king in battle, Bestowing the ploughman's fort, Coming as a maternal uncle to arbitrate, Showing Shivlokam to Varaguna Pandiyan, Selling firewood, Giving a sacred letter, Placing a plank, Winning a music debate, Giving milk to piglings, Making piglings ministers, Teaching a crow, Granting liberation to a stork, Becoming Thiru Aalavaai, Shooting the beautiful arrow, Giving the Sangam plank, Giving a golden purse to Dharumi, Rescuing Keeran, Teaching grammar to Keeran, Resolving the dispute of the Sangam poets, Resolving Idaippadan's displeasure, Casting a net, Teaching Vaathavoor Adigal, Turning foxes into horses, Turning horses into foxes, Carrying earth, Curing the Pandya king's fever, Impaling the Jains, Invoking the Vanni tree, well, and Lingam. These are the 64 Thiruvilaiyadals performed by the Lord in Madurai. We will continue to contemplate the history of Murthi Nayanar, who was born and lived in such a glorious city of Madurai, through the Periya Puranam. Thiruchitrambalam. Adagam Madurai Arase Potri, Koodal Ilangu Kurumani Potri. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 05 — Murthi Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 184, Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Murthi Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing about the glories of Madhurapuri. Madurai is an ancient city. Its antiquity is evident from Sangam literature and other historical accounts. This ancient city is sung by Sekkizhar Peruman as "Saalpu aay mummai Tamil thangiya angann mootoor" (The ancient city where the three forms of Tamil, which teach perfection, reside). Saalpu means complete excellence. Its meaning is the three forms of Tamil that instruct beings on the nature of perfection. Mummai Tamil means the three types of Tamil: Iyal (prose/literature), Isai (music), and Natakam (drama). Thus, Madurai is a beautiful ancient city where the three forms of Tamil, which impart the nature of perfection and wisdom to beings, reside. Here, there are many Sangams (academies) that research poetic works and present the best ones on stage. Women in the mansions of Madurai city play ball, adorned with pearl necklaces. Women playing ball while singing has been one of the ancient customs of Tamils. Playing ball is one of the games for women. Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing about Madurai. The verse is: "Mummai bhuvanangalin mikkadhan reyem mootoor meymmai porulaam Tamil noolin vilangu vaaimai semmai porulum tharuvaar Thiru Aalavaayil emmai bhavam theerppavar sangam irundhadhendraal." Madurai is a very ancient city. It is superior to the three worlds: upper, middle, and lower. It is an ancient city that appeared before all other cities. This is because it is the place where the primordial God, who appeared and manifested before everyone, is enshrined. Therefore, it is an ancient city. Tamil is the Tamil that contains true philosophical texts. Tamil texts have the nature of distinguishing between true and false realities and establishing the world. The Lord Himself, as a poet in the Sangam here, has revealed the truth and profound meaning that shines in these Tamil texts. Sivakavimani Ayya offers various interpretations for the phrase "Tamil noolin vilangu vaaimai semmai porulum tharuvaar Thiru Aalavaayil emmai bhavam theerppavar" in this verse. First, let's look at the meaning of "Tamil noolin vilangu vaaimai semmai porul." The primary meaning is that the texts are those that reveal the ultimate truth, the profound meaning that shines in Tamil texts. Here, "meymmai porul" refers to the state of certainty of truth, and "vaaimai semmai porul" within it refers to the yogic liberation attained through experiential realization of that certain truth. Next, taking "semmai porul" to mean "semporul" (ultimate truth), it signifies the truth that bestows liberation. The experiential state of liberation, which is the ultimate truth, is conveyed by Tamil Akapporul (love poetry) texts. This is clearly explained by the prefaces to Thirukkovaiyar and Iraiyanar Agapporul. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman sings, "Thiru Aalavaayil Iraivar, who bestows the truth and profound meaning that shines in the true philosophical Tamil texts." The Lord in Thiru Aalavaai explained the true nature of Tamil texts. This can be known from Iraiyanar Agapporul and histories like Thiruvilaiyadal Puranam's "Sangathar Kazhagam Theerthathu." "Semmai porulum tharuvaar" means that He bestows not only the knowledge of scriptures and worldly affairs, but also the subtle truth of Jnana Yoga, the experiential state of liberation, which is difficult for anyone else to convey through the path of Agamic texts. "Thannar Tamil alikkum thanpaandi naattanaai" is from Thiruvachagam, Thiru Ammanai. Lord Shiva, as Iraiyanar, not only gave Tamil texts (words) here in Madurai in the Sangam, but also gave their meaning. Its meaning is that He bestows gold, true wealth, worldly pleasures, and liberation. The Agamas state this as "bhoga apavarggadam." That is why, in the subsequent phrase, Sekkizhar Peruman praises the Lord as "emmai bhavam theerppavar" (He who removes our sins). "Sangam" (academy) implies the giving of texts, and "bhavam theerppavar" (remover of sins) implies the giving of ultimate truth. "Iraiyanar, the remover of sins, was in the Sangam" means He presided as the head of the Sangam. Thus, the Lord Himself, as a Sangam poet and head of the Sangam here, revealed texts that convey truth and profound meaning. He also revealed the meaning of those texts. Sekkizhar Peruman, who came to sing the glories of the Pandya country and Madurai, intertwines Tamil with the Pandya country and Madurai. There are many countries within the boundaries of Tamil Nadu. Among them, only the Pandya country is called Tamil Nadu. The Lord and His divine son, along with Agastya, who understood Tamil, were in the Sangam and nurtured Tamil. The idea is that Agastya still spreads Tamil from Podhigai. Therefore, just as the Chola country and the Kaveri River are inseparable in our thoughts, so too are the Pandya country and Tamil inseparable in our thoughts. That is why Sekkizhar Peruman, when singing about the Pandya country and Madurai, intertwines Tamil with them. The author has shown how our great Acharyas worshipped the Lord of Aalavaai as the head of Tamil in their respective Puranas. Sivakavimani Ayya writes in his commentary that in such a glorious Pandya country, the fragrance of Tamil is greatly diminishing these days, and that tragic sight is regrettable. Here, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that Tamil provides texts filled with truth. But in later and present times, besides divine texts, which are called truth in Tamil, other kinds of texts are now emerging. Furthermore, many heterodox religious texts like Jainism and Buddhism, which were rejected by our great Acharyas, are also in Tamil. If such doubts arise, Sekkizhar Peruman has stated here that there are Tamil texts that are called true texts, which have Lord Shiva as their inner meaning. Sekkizhar Peruman considered and acknowledged only such texts as Tamil texts. He did not consider Tamil texts that discuss other matters as texts at all. Therefore, the firm conclusion of our sages is that Tamil texts are those that speak only of the Lord and of truth. The inner meaning is that sages will not accept or acknowledge other texts as texts at all. In such a glorious Madurai, Murthi Nayanar was born as a result of the penance performed by a distinguished family in the merchant community. Murthi Nayanar was born into the merchant community. The family he was born into was a noble family. Kulam is a large division. Kudi is a smaller division within it. Here, "Seer Kudi" refers to a great family, one of the three types of families within the merchant community. Sekkizhar Peruman sings of this family as "Aandra Thonmai Kulam." "Aandra Thonmai Kulam" means an ancient family that has been known as a good family in the Vedas and other dharmic texts from time immemorial. This is because the merchant community is one of the four types of communities classified by the Dharma Shastras. In such a family, in a noble lineage, Murthi Nayanar was born due to the great penance performed by that lineage. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to his birth as "Sei Thavam Seyya Vandhar" (He came to perform penance). The first meaning of this is that he was born here due to the penance performed by that lineage. There is also a second meaning: that he came to perform the penance that ought to be performed, which is Saivite penance. For the Sivagnanabodham sutra "Thavam Seytha Narchaarbil Vandhuthiththu" (Born in a good lineage that performed penance), Madhava Sivagnana Yogigal has commented, "Born in a high lineage suitable for performing penance again." Therefore, its meaning is that he was born in a lineage that performed penance, in order to perform penance again. Murthi Nayanar was one who severed all attachments, both internal (I, mine) and external. He held the sacred feet of Lord Shiva as his true attachment, loving them with great devotion. By stating that Lord Shiva's sacred feet are the true attachment, it is implied that all other attachments are false. "Patruga patratraan patrinai appatrai patruga patru vidarku" is from Thirukkural. "Matru patru enakkinri nin thirupathame manam paviththen" is from Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thevaram. "Patrai arruppadhor patrinai pattrila patrai arruppar endru undhi parai" is from Thiruundhiyar. "Patrinul patrai thudaippadhor patrarindhu patrap pirindhirundhu paarkindra patradhanai patruvidil annilaiye thaan paramagum" is from Thirukkalitruppadiyar. As stated in all these divine utterances, Murthi Nayanar was one who severed all other attachments and clung to the sacred feet of Lord Shiva as his true attachment. Among the 63 Nayanmars, two embraced asceticism. Murthi Nayanar is one of them. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman first describes his detachment, which was the cause of his asceticism. For all souls, the Lord's sacred feet are the refuge for this world and the next. Thus, the Lord's golden feet are those that first bestow bondage upon souls, then grant liberation, and then supreme liberation. Murthi Nayanar had an unceasing and immense desire for the golden feet of Lord Shiva, who bestows bondage and liberation upon souls and takes them into His grace. That great desire transformed into great love. It became a yearning, a longing. Thus, his love for the Lord grew and overflowed, becoming a form itself. His sacred name is Murthiyar. Murthi means body, sacred form, or shape. The one who resides within a form and animates it is Murthimaan. Our Nayanar, as we have already seen, was one whose love grew and overflowed, becoming a form, a Murthi. Therefore, he received the sacred name Murthiyar. Murthi Nayanar was engaged in the sacred service of grinding sandalwood paste for the anointing of Lord Aalavaaiyudaiyar, our Father Lord Shiva, who resides in Thiru Aalavaai. He was performing the sacred service of grinding sandalwood for the Lord's anointing. "Sandhanamum kungumamum santhum thoyndha tholanaai" is from Thiruththandaagam. Among the substances used for the Lord's sacred bath, sandalwood paste is considered excellent, according to Agamic rules. Furthermore, sandalwood is used for anointing and applying tilak. The most superior sandalwood is red sandalwood, also known as Malacca sandalwood. The 34th verse in the Maruthavarai Padalam of Perur Puranam sings about this Malacca sandalwood: "Kaasil panneer pachchai karpooram kungumappoo maasil urosanai pulugu maan madha naaviyum kalandha vaasigandha malaakka sandhana milaippana madhanai thesavirun thirumeni muzhudhum serithara kottal." Its meaning is: "Pure rosewater, green camphor, saffron, gorochana, civet, musk, and deer musk mixed with Malacca sandalwood is the anointing paste. This should be applied all over the Lord's radiant divine body." Thus, our Murthi Nayanar is performing the sacred service of grinding sandalwood for Lord Sokkalinga, who resides in Aalavaai. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 06 — Murthi Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 185, Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Murthi Nayanar Puranam. In Madurai, our Murthi Nayanar is performing the sacred service of grinding sandalwood for the anointing and sacred bath of Lord Sokkalinga. He performs this service continuously, without missing even a single day. Our Nayanar performs this sacred service because it is very pleasing to the Lord, because Shiva's devotees will be very happy with it, and because it is dear to his own heart. During these days, a Vaduga Karnataka king invades the Pandya country. Sekkizhar Peruman mentions "Vaduga Karnataka Nadu" here. "Vaduga" refers to a language. This name came to denote the country where that language is spoken. This country is the northern border of the Tamil country. Furthermore, "Vaduga" is also a name given to the people living there. "Vadathisai marungin vaduga varambaaga" is from Tholkappiyam Sutra Urai. Thus, this Vaduga country is the northern border of Tamil Nadu. Sekkizhar Peruman mentions "Vaduga Karnadar." "Karnadar" means people of Karnataka. This is also used as a language name. "Karunadam" evolved into "Kannadam." This country has land with black clay soil, hence it was named "Karunadu" (black country). This is the opinion of linguists. Furthermore, there is reason to believe that this country was once a Tamil-speaking country, and that the Vaduga language and Kannada language originated from Tamil. Also, Karnataka is considered one of the 56 countries of Bharata Khanda. Therefore, "Vaduga Karnadar" probably refers to the Kannada region populated by Vadugas. This country naturally had forests as its defense. A forest is an important fortification for a country. The definition of a fort includes height, breadth, strength, and inaccessibility – all of which are found in a forest. Such a country, with forests as its defense, is the Vaduga Karnataka country. Therefore, it is historically known that these Vadugas from the North invaded the South and Tamil Nadu at various times, conquering and seizing many places. The rule of those kings who invaded in this manner also fell after some time. Even after their fall, these people did not return to their own country but dispersed and lived in various parts of Tamil Nadu. These people who came and settled here mingled with the native Tamil people, shared the land, and became rooted, living like the people of this country. The nature of the Karnataka country is black clay soil. Therefore, wherever there was black clay soil, they settled and lived. Thus, their descendants can be found everywhere in our country where there is clay soil, as known from the history of the land. Thus, the Vaduga Karnataka king from the North invaded and waged war against the Pandya country. He defeated the Pandya king. Then he made Madurai his capital and began to rule. Generally, in our Bharata Desam, there is a tradition of warfare. If a king invades another country and conquers it, he would allow the defeated king to continue ruling that country, collect only tribute from him, consider that king as a feudatory under his own rule, and let him rule there, collecting tribute. This was the general tradition in Bharata Desam. But the Karnataka king who invaded here was driven by immense greed and desire for land. Therefore, after defeating the Pandya king, instead of allowing the Pandya king to rule under his suzerainty and collecting tribute, according to the Bharata Desam war tradition, he himself crowned himself king of Madurai and began to rule. Even though he won the war, the Vaduga Karnataka king, who should have respected the rule that Tamil Nadu should be ruled by a Tamil king, allowed the Tamil king to rule, and carried away tribute as a symbol of his victory, did not do so. Instead, he crowned himself king and began to rule. Even the Chola kings, when they conquered countries up to the Himalayas, planted their tiger flag, and subjugated the North Indian kings, even after defeating them, allowed them to rule there and only collected tribute, living as emperors. But this Karnataka king, who cared little for war ethics or governance ethics, himself became king of Madurai. Tamil Nadu is a generous country. The Tamil language is a generous language. Why is the Tamil language generous? Because it has the nature of providing true meaning and pleasures to those who seek and learn it, without any deficiency, as they desire. Therefore, Tamil is a generous language. Similarly, Tamil Nadu is a generous country. The strength of a country is that it should provide wealth without being sought. Thirukkural states: "Naadenba naada valaththana naadalla naada valam tharum naadu." Its meaning is that a country should provide wealth to its inhabitants without them having to search for it. Such a country is Tamil Nadu. Tamil Nadu is a country that naturally produces wealth. Nevertheless, a king's duty is to further increase artificial wealth in such a naturally rich country using its natural resources and make it useful to the people. Increasing artificial wealth on top of natural wealth is one of the duties of a government. Cutting down forests to create countries, digging canals to increase crops, etc., are examples of this. These are all duties of a government. But that Karnataka king did not even perform the duty of developing such a naturally rich country. Not only that, but the Karnataka king who crowned himself in Madurai did not follow the Saiva religion, which is associated with sacred ash, but was under the influence of Jains and was a Jain king. While there was the entirely beneficial path of Saivism, the righteous path of sacred ash, he fell into the entirely harmful path of the Arhats, Jainism. He fell thus due to ignorance. What is knowledge? It is the knowledge that distinguishes between what is impermanent and what is permanent, and recognizes the permanent true reality as "this." This is called Nithya Anithya Vasthu Vivekam in Sanskrit. Vivekam means the knowledge that distinguishes between what is eternal and what is non-eternal. This Vivekam was absent in that Karnataka king. Therefore, he abandoned the path of sacred ash, which is Sat (truth), and embraced Jainism, which is Asat (untruth), and fell under the influence of Jains. In this verse explaining this, Sekkizhar Peruman describes Saivism as "Meruvillan adimaiththiram meviya neetrin saarbu" (the path of sacred ash that embodies the devotion to the Lord who wielded Meru as a bow). Sivakavimani beautifully explains this. Here, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Lord Shiva as the Lord who bent Meru as a bow. Lord Shiva bent Meru as a bow to burn the three cities. Then, with His laughter, He burned the three cities. Not only that, but He also granted Shiva's grace (Shiva Perru) to the three devotees of Shiva in those three cities. Therefore, our Lord Shiva is the one who simultaneously brought destruction to the three cities and granted the state of Shiva's grace to the three devotees living in those three cities. In this Murthi Nayanar Puranam as well, Lord Shiva is about to simultaneously bestow His grace for the fall of this Karnataka Jain king and the coronation of our Murthi Nayanar as a Saivite king in Madurai. Sekkizhar Peruman has used this beautiful phrase to convey this underlying meaning, which Sivakavimani Ayya points out to us. Furthermore, here Sekkizhar Peruman, when referring to the Saiva path, calls it "Thiruneetru saarbaagiya Saiva neri" (the Saiva path associated with sacred ash). Sacred ash is a means that guides souls in devotion to Lord Shiva and establishes them in His service. It is also a symbol that signifies devotion to Lord Shiva. It serves as medicine to cure the disease of birth that afflicts the soul, and also as a preventive medicine against diseases. Its greatness can be seen in the Thiruneetru Pathigam (decad on sacred ash) composed by Aaludaiya Pillaiyar. This sacred ash is especially dear to the Lord of Thiru Aalavaai, as known from the concluding line "Thiru Aalavaayaan Thiruneere" (the sacred ash of the Lord of Thiru Aalavaai) in that decad, sung by Thirugnanasambandha Peruman. Later, in the history of Thirugnanasambandha Peruman, when the hunchbacked Pandya king of Madurai, due to his past evil karma, had embraced Jainism, Thirugnanasambandha Peruman, who guided him to devotion to Shiva, performed that act using sacred ash, as we shall see later. Here, in the Murthi Nayanar Puranam as well, we are going to see the history of our Murthi Nayanar taking sacred ash as his anointing substance as a symbol of Jainism receding and Saivism flourishing. Therefore, here too, Sekkizhar Peruman has sung about the Saiva path by associating it with sacred ash. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 07 — Murthi Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 186, Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Murthi Nayanar Puranam. We saw the news of the Karnataka king, who invaded from the north, being crowned as the king of Madurai. We also saw that he fell into Jainism. Jainism is also called Arugar Samayam. Arugan means the god of Jainism. Those who consider him as God are called Aruganthar. The king, instead of adhering to the Saiva religion that flourished in the Pandya country and living by the path of sacred ash, embraced Jainism and succumbed to their evil thoughts. That king was a cruel person who fell into the path of the deceitful Jains, considering their false penances as true. He considered the penance of the Jains as true penance. The penances of the Jains include lying on hot rocks, practicing non-violence, wandering naked, plucking hair from the head, eating while standing without speaking, and refraining from eating at night. Believing all these to be true penance, the king fell into the trap of the Jains. He became a thief who seized unripe fruit while ripe fruit was available. He embraced unrighteousness while righteousness existed. He performed evil deeds and became arrogant while penance was available. Not only did he fall into the path of the Jains, abandoning the path of sacred ash of Lord Aalavaai, but he also tormented Shiva's devotees and Shiva bhaktas in Madurai, committing a great wrong and thereby incurring great sin. Here, Sekkizhar Peruman, when referring to Shiva's devotees, calls them devotees of Shiva, who has a matted lock where a serpent and a radiant moon reside. In the Lord's matted lock, there will be both a serpent and a moon. "Pambodu thingal pagai theerththu aandaai" is from Thiruththandaagam. According to that, even though the serpent and the moon are enemies to each other, Lord Shiva has arranged them to live together in His matted lock. Why are the serpent and the moon enemies? It is our tradition to metaphorically say that the serpent is an enemy to the moon because the moon is eclipsed by Rahu and Ketu, who belong to the serpent's lineage. Nevertheless, both live harmoniously in the Lord's sacred matted lock. Sekkizhar Peruman has included this detail here to show that this nature of Lord Shiva, who made the serpent and the moon, who were hostile, live together, indicates that Shiva's devotees also possess such a nature. This means that Saivite devotees, without any conflict of "this is it, that is not," will not bear enmity towards anything, but will keep everything in its proper place and remain calm. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman says "vaazhum sadaiyaan" (He with the living matted lock) and "adiyaaraiyum" (and His devotees) to indicate that harming such devotees, persecuting them, is improper even for enemies. Furthermore, that king tormented Shiva's devotees, unaware that offending Shiva's devotees is a greater sin than offending Shiva himself. The books like "Siva Dharmottaram" state that those who harm Shiva's devotees will suffer in hell. The fact that those who cause Shiva's devotees to be distressed will soon be destroyed, and that Appar Swamigal, in his Kala Parayana Thirukkurunthogai, commanded Yama's messengers not to go near the devotees, indicates the nature of Shiva's devotees. Shiva's devotees are those with great qualities who do not bear enmity anywhere. Even if they belong to opposing religions, they consider them as paths revealed by Lord Shiva, assign those religions their respective places, and live without conflict or enmity with anyone. But such Shiva's devotees are tormented by Jains. It is stated here that Shiva's devotees do not show enmity towards anyone. At this point, a doubt might arise in us. Our great Acharya Swamigal, Thirugnanasambandha Peruman, debated and defeated the Jains. Our other great Acharya, Manikkavasaga Swamigal, debated and defeated the Buddhists. Appar Swamigal and Thirugnanasambandha Peruman have disparaged Jains and Buddhists. Even in this history, in Murthi Nayanar Puranam, Murthiyar thinks, "When will the day come when this Jain sinner perishes and Jainism disappears?" Such questions might arise in us. All these are true. Jains and Buddhists, who belonged to heterodox religions, entered our Saivite country and, instead of living peacefully within their own limits, tormented us Saivites, seized Saivite lands, and caused obstacles to the Vedic path of Saivism. Therefore, our great Acharya Swamigal performed such divine acts to remove these Jain and Buddhist obstacles to the Vedic and Agamic path of Saivism, to make the Vedic path flourish, to make the abundant Saivite tradition shine, to protect Saivism from Jain and Buddhist obstacles, and to establish the truth. They performed these actions having received the Lord's divine command. They did not perform these actions out of mere enmity – that is the truth. Deceit, envy, etc., are evil qualities. These arise from the quality of Tamas. That Jain king, who harbored such Tamasic qualities in his heart, committed cruelties by tormenting Murthiyar, a devotee of Lord Shiva, the three-eyed one, enshrined in Thiru Aalavaai. Even though he caused such troubles, our Murthi Nayanar continued his service of grinding sandalwood without fail. Our Nayanmar great souls are those who, even when poverty and weariness befall them in worldly life, never abandon their sacred service concerning the well-being of the soul, but perform it regularly. Here, Murthi Nayanar also performed his sacred service regularly, even amidst great difficulties. The reason for his unfailing performance of the Lord's service is his immense love for the Lord. Sekkizhar Peruman poses a question and answers it: "Who can obstruct the sacred service of such glorious devotees and our great souls?" The king, who engaged in actions condemned by dharmic texts and noble persons, blocked all routes through which sandalwood logs, essential for Murthi Nayanar's service of grinding sandalwood paste, arrived. He prevented Murthi Nayanar from obtaining sandalwood logs. Sandalwood logs could be purchased. Sandalwood trees could be cut from groves. The king blocked all such routes for sandalwood trees. He prevented Murthi Nayanar from obtaining sandalwood logs through his usual means. He severely punished those who sold sandalwood logs. He prevented trees from being cut in sandalwood groves. Such actions are contrary to royal justice and righteous rule. Lord Shiva bestows grace to people of different religions according to their respective paths. In all the ways people of different religions worship, Lord Shiva Himself stands and bestows grace upon them. A king who is skilled in righteous rule, which is the justice ordained by Shiva, should similarly uphold the respective religious tenets of his subjects in his country and protect those paths. That is royal justice. Deviating from it, as the king of Madurai did here, is tyrannical rule. Our author Sekkizhar Peruman, a great minister skilled in royal justice, explains this to us so that the world may understand this justice and live accordingly. When the king blocked all routes for sandalwood logs, our Murthi Nayanar was distressed. Devotees of Shiva are those who lack nothing, are brave and glorious, and are without fear or worry. Murthi Nayanar is also a noble devotee in that assembly of devotees. Nevertheless, here Murthi Nayanar, being a devotee, was distressed. This is because he feared that his sacred service to the Lord would be obstructed, and thus he was distressed. Being a devotee who loved Lord Shiva, he was distressed that an obstacle had come to his service due to his love for the Lord's feet. Thirumoola Deva Nayanar has stated what kind of harm will come if the hearts of Shiva's devotees are distressed. In the tenth Thirumurai, Thirumoola Deva Nayanar's Thirumandhiram, in the second Thanthiram, under the heading "Guru Nindhai Koodaamai" (Not to insult the Guru), there are verses. The second verse among them is as follows: "Paththini paththargal thaththuva gnanigal siththam kalanga chithaivugal seythavar aththamum aaviyum aandu onril maanddidum saththiyam eedhu sadhanandhi aanaiye." This is that verse. Its meaning is: If chaste women (paththini pengal), Shiva's devotees, and those with philosophical knowledge have their minds distressed and their paths destroyed, their wealth and lifespan will perish within one year. This is the truth, by the oath of our Nandi Peruman, says Thirumoolar Peruman. Thirumoolar Peruman has listed chaste women, Shiva's devotees, and those with philosophical knowledge here. These three types of people – chaste women, Shiva's devotees, and those with philosophical knowledge – even if they are not direct gurus to people, they make this world stand on the path of righteousness through their conduct. Therefore, they become teachers to the people of the world. Therefore, harming them is equivalent to harming the world itself. Hence, the harm caused by harming these three is much greater than harming anyone else, explains Thirumoolar Peruman. Therefore, it is understood that the same harm that comes from insulting one's spiritual guru will come if harm is caused to these three categories of people. Furthermore, Thirumoolar Peruman, in the second Thanthiram of Thirumandhiram, under the heading "Maheswara Nindhai Koodaamai" (Not to insult Maheswara), has sung: "Eesan adiyaar idhayam kalangida desamum naadum sirappum azhindhidum vaasavan peedamum mann maamannar peedamum naasamathu aayidum nam Nandi aanaiye." Its meaning is: If the heart of a Shiva devotee is pained for any reason, the country, nation, and its glory that caused it will be destroyed. Not only that, but the rule of the king of heaven and the throne of the great king of earth will also be destroyed. Therefore, the Karnataka king who came from the north and ruled in Madurai committed an improper act, causing the heart of Shiva's devotees to be distressed. He caused our Murthi Nayanar's heart to be pained. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 08 — Murthi Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 187, Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Murthi Nayanar Puranam. The Karnataka king, who was crowned in Madurai, fell into the Jain religion and caused suffering to Shiva's devotees and Murthi Nayanar. He blocked all routes through which sandalwood logs, needed for Murthi Nayanar's service of grinding sandalwood, arrived. Due to this, our Murthi Nayanar was distressed in mind. Sekkizhar Peruman composes a verse describing what Murthi Nayanar thought in his mind: "Punmai chcheyal val aman kundarir podhu pokkum vanmai kodum paathagan maayndhida vaaimai vedha nanmai thiruneetru uyar nalneri thaangum menmai thanmai puvi mannarai chchaarvadhen endru saarvaar." When will this earth receive a king of noble character who upholds the exalted righteous path of sacred ash, which bestows goodness as declared in the true Vedas, after this cruel sinner, who wastes his time with the base and powerful Jain thugs, perishes? Murthi Nayanar ponders this in his mind. The Jains are referred to as "Punmai chcheyal val aman kundar" by Sekkizhar Swamigal in this verse. "Punmai chcheyal" means base actions, evil deeds. "Punmai chcheyal val aman kundar" means that Jains are skilled in performing base actions. "Kundar" means low-class people. We are well aware of the harms inflicted by Jains on Thirunavukkarasu Swamigal. They deceitfully slandered Thirunavukkarasu Peruman to the king of that country. With the king's command, they put Thirunavukkarasu Peruman in a lime kiln. They made him eat poisoned milk-rice. They let loose an elephant to trample him. They tied him to a stone and threw him into the sea. We must remember that Jains committed all these harms to Thirunavukkarasu Peruman. In Madurai, they set fire to the monastery where Thirugnanasambandha Peruman was staying at night. They insulted Dandiyadigal, used force, and distressed his mind. They spoke disparagingly to Naminandi Nayanar and pained his heart. We must recall that Jains committed all such base actions. Because Jains committed such actions, Appar Swamigal, who was compassionate and never caused harm to any living being at any time, even he, with his pure divine mind, was distressed and referred to them as "Aman Kundar" and "Kundakkar" in his divine utterances. The king wasted his time associating with such a Jain group. The king, whose duty was to guide his country on the right path, elevate himself, and save his subjects, wasted his time with Jains and caused such suffering. Murthi Nayanar wished that such a king, who committed the great sin of offending Shiva, should die, and thereby Saivism, the righteous path proclaimed by the Vedas, should flourish. The heterodox religion, the Pāṣaṇḍa religion of Jainism, which rejects our Vedas, should fall. The Jain king should fall. The Vedas state "Satyameva Jayate" in the Mundaka Upanishad. The Vedas declare that truth alone is firm, truth alone triumphs. Murthi Nayanar wished that Saivism, which is associated with sacred ash and is glorified and specially spoken of by such Vedas, should flourish. He longed for the day when this country would receive a king belonging to such a Saiva religion. Because "Mannan evvazhi mannuyir avvazhi" (As the king, so the people) is a proverb. Its meaning is that the world depends on the king. Therefore, our Murthi Nayanar longed for the arrival of a Saiva king. As the Karnataka Jain king blocked all routes for sandalwood logs, Murthi Nayanar could not find sandalwood logs anywhere throughout the day. Due to this, deeply disheartened and saddened, Murthi Nayanar arrives at the temple where his Lord Sokkalinga Peruman is enshrined. Sekkizhar Peruman composes the next verse: "Nattam purivaar ani nar siru meyppoochchu indru muttum parisaayinum theykkum kai muttaththadhendru vattam thigazh paaraiyin vaiththu muzhangai theyththaar. Kattum puram thol narambenbu karaindhu theyya." An obstacle has arisen today for the sacred service of anointing the Lord who performs the divine dance. Nevertheless, there is no obstacle to my hand that grinds the sandalwood. Thus, Murthi Nayanar, with resolve, placed his elbow on the round sandalwood grinding stone and rubbed it until his outer skin, nerves, and bones wore away. He described the anatomical parts – skin, nerves, bones, flesh, blood – which are enclosed within the elbow, in the order they would wear away from outside to inside when rubbed on a stone: first the outer skin, then the nerves, then the bones. Sekkizhar Peruman composes the next verse: "Kallin puram theyththa muzhangai kallundu sori sellum parapponganum enbu thirandhu moolai pullumbadi kandu poruththilar thampiraanaar. Allin kan ezhundhadhu vandharul seytha vaakku." The elbow, rubbed on the stone, bled profusely, and wherever it was rubbed, the bone opened up, and the brain matter within became visible. Seeing this, Lord Shiva, his Master, could not bear it. The Lord could not tolerate it. That very night, His divine voice of grace arose. Inside the elbow, there are two flat, long bones. They are like delicate, hollow tubes. Inside those hollows, the subtle, nerve-like brain matter is filled. To protect this, the bones are like guarding tubes. When the hard, white outer part of the bones wore away, those bone tubes opened, and the inner part was exposed. When this was exposed, the Lord could not bear it. The Lord's grace manifests when the ego of "I" and the possessiveness of "mine," which are internal and external attachments, are completely absent. As long as there is even a slight attachment, He observes patiently. But when all these attachments are gone, and attachment to the body and soul is completely removed, He cannot bear it and manifests His grace. "Attradhu pattrenil uttradhu veedu" (When attachment is severed, liberation is attained) is a proverb. Therefore, here the Lord speaks His divine word. He bestows grace upon Murthiyar. The divine voice arose from the all-pervading divine form of the Lord, the all-pervading sky of knowledge, for Murthiyar to hear. We have already seen how, for Arivattaya Nayanar, the Lord's divine hand appeared from the crack in the earth and stopped him. For Kannappa Nayanar, the Lord's divine hand emerged from the Shiva Lingam. In the Purana of Thirukkuripth Thonda Nayanar, which we are about to see, the Lord will emerge from a rock and bestow grace upon Thirukkuripth Thonda Nayanar. Similarly, here, for Murthi Nayanar, the Lord bestows grace through His divine utterance. The Lord, speaking through His divine voice, addressed Murthi Nayanar, saying, "O noble one, you did this act out of the courage of love. Do not do so anymore. This entire country, which he forcefully seized and caused you great suffering, will now come to you. Remove all past troubles, protect this country, rule it, perform your regular sacred service, and finally attain our supreme world." Thus, the Lord bestowed grace upon Murthi Nayanar in the form of a divine utterance. The Lord first addresses Murthi Nayanar as "Ayya" (noble one). The Lord, pleased with his act of love, affectionately calls Murthi Nayanar thus. "Ayyan" means one who is excellent in greatness, a sage. The Lord says, "You did this out of the courage of love." The courage Murthi Nayanar showed, thinking, "Even if there is an obstacle to the anointing service today, there is no obstacle to the hand that grinds," is praised by the Lord as the courage of love. There are some who torment their own bodies for various worldly reasons like enmity, worry, conflict, etc. But the Lord says to Murthi Nayanar, "You did this not for any such reason, but out of immense love for Me, because there was an obstacle to My sandalwood anointing." Furthermore, Sekkizhar Peruman has composed this verse using the past tense "konda mann" (the land he seized) to indicate that the king's lifespan was over and he would die. The Lord says that the Nayanar himself will take that land, meaning that country, because the king has died. It is conveyed here that Murthi Nayanar will become king of the country that lies without a king. Murthi Nayanar had previously grieved, wishing for the death of the wicked and the arrival of a good king, had he not? Therefore, Lord Sokkanathar of Aalavaai, who grants whatever His devotees desire, bestowed upon Murthi Nayanar exactly what he desired. Therefore, the Lord says, "You yourself be king, protect the world without falling into evil or deviating from the righteous path, safeguard Saivism so that it flourishes, perform your sacred service, and then come to our eternal Shiva Puri, the world of liberation." Here, we can see the administrative skill of Sekkizhar Peruman, who composed this verse to convey the truth that "the penance of great ascetics and the chastity of women do not endure without the protection of a protector." Hearing this divine utterance of the Lord, Murthi Nayanar was first afraid. This is because the Lord said, "O beloved, do not do this act," did He not? He fears that he has done the act that the Lord told him not to do. Thus, fearing, he stops that act. He stops the act of rubbing his hand. The worn wound on Murthi Nayanar's hand healed, and the colors of the Nayanar's hand, imbued with all good fragrances, became beautiful and complete. The wound on Murthi Nayanar's sacred hand was caused by rubbing his elbow on the sandalwood stone, which wore away his outer skin and nerves and abraded his bone. All those wounds healed by the Lord's grace, and Murthi Nayanar's sacred hand became beautiful, possessing all the qualities of roundness, firmness, beauty, and strength, just as it was before. Sacred texts state that those who perform Shiva's service attain the positions appropriate to that service. Our Murthi Nayanar was performing the sacred service of grinding sandalwood, which is fragrant, for the Lord's anointing. Accordingly, Sekkizhar Swamigal sings that the Lord blessed him to attain radiance imbued with all fragrances. "Vaasam eythinaar" (attained fragrance) refers to the human body losing its fleshy odor and acquiring a divine fragrance. Murthi Nayanar rubbed his hand, considering it an equivalent to the sandalwood log used for the Lord's anointing. Therefore, the Lord also restores his sacred hand to be fragrant with a divine scent, like sandalwood. Furthermore, Murthi Nayanar attained the light of true knowledge, which is the manifestation of Shivattva, the right to knowledge. Murthi Nayanar, having embraced complete asceticism, therefore attained a divine body with abundant light of knowledge. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 09 — Murthi Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 188, Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Murthi Nayanar Puranam. We saw Lord Shiva bestowing grace upon Murthi Nayanar. Generally, the lifespan of this body is as brief as a flash of lightning. It is a well-known truth that the time people's bodies remain in this world is very short. This body is called "mey" (truth/body). This is because it has the nature of appearing real but disappearing as false. Therefore, wise people refer to it as "mey" as a matter of convention. Thus, the impermanence of this body is a general rule. But for those who are not devoted to Shankara, this impermanence is even greater. As per Thirumoola Deva Nayanar's Thirumandhiram, which we saw earlier, the wealth and life of those who harm Shiva's devotees will perish within one year. According to this divine utterance, the lifespan of those who torment Shiva's devotees will end even more quickly. This is a special rule. If the life of ordinary people ends quickly, then the life of those who are not devoted to Shiva ends even more quickly – this is its explanation. Therefore, it is deduced through arthapatti (presumption) that the lifespan of Shiva's devotees will be prolonged. The history of Sage Markandeya is proof of this. Furthermore, Thirugnanasambandha Peruman's Kolaru Thiruppathigam, verse four, states: "Kodhiyuru kaalan anggi namanodu thoodhar kodu noykalaana palavum athi guna nalla nalla avai nalla nalla adiyaar avarkku migave." We must also recall the fate of Daksha, who not only lacked devotion to Shiva but also harmed Shiva's devotees and committed offenses against Shiva. Therefore, this Karnataka king, not only failed to adhere to the path of Shiva but also caused suffering to Shiva's devotees. Thus, according to Lord Shiva's divine utterance, he died that very night. His days were over. Just as good people who serve Shiva's devotees in this world attain Shiva's world very quickly, so too do those who torment Shiva's devotees fall into hell just as quickly. After separating from this body, souls go to births appropriate to the good and bad deeds they have done. If past karmas are abundant, they are sent to meritorious or sinful worlds according to their respective karmas, experience corresponding pleasures and pains, and then return to birth. Good people who have accumulated abundant Shiva punya, which is the highest among good deeds, do not go to the two types of worlds mentioned above (heaven and hell) or other worlds, but immediately attain Shiva's world, where they do not experience rebirth. Those who have committed grave sins, such as offenses against Shiva, are not immediately reborn but are cast into terrible great hells for a long time. Thus, that tyrannical king, who was completely swayed by the quality of Tamas, died. That king, referred to by Sekkizhar Peruman as wicked, deceitful, tyrannical, sinful, cruel, one who caused severe wounds, and evil, died as per Lord Shiva's earlier divine utterance and fell into terrible hell. Since he died very quickly, his wife and relatives were distressed and saddened, unable to comprehend the suddenness of the event, like being struck by lightning, in an utterly unexpected situation. Just as night follows evening and then dawn breaks, that night passed and the next morning arrived. The red-rayed sun, Surya, rose. The association with Shiva is like the lord of the Pandya country. During the reign of this tyrannical king, the Pandya country struggled, unable to attain its lord, the association with Shiva. Therefore, the period of the tyrannical king's rule, which was daytime for him, was a dark night for the Pandya country. Now that the tyrannical king has fallen, the dark age of the Pandya country has ended, and the red-rayed sun, Surya, has risen. After the death of that Tamasic king, Murthi Nayanar is about to arrive, like the rising sun. As dawn broke, the king had died. Therefore, the ministers, who were the king's limbs and managed state affairs, all gathered to discuss how the government should proceed. The deceased king was a Jain. Therefore, the chief ministers gathered and performed the king's funeral rites according to Jain customs. It is a dharma for everyone to perform the funeral rites for the deceased, whoever they may be, in their proper manner. Therefore, the ministers quickly completed those funeral rites. The king died without the fate of a good life. He had no son to succeed him in the lineage of kingship. Therefore, the ministers consult on how to find a king when there is no king from the royal lineage to protect this land. In ancient times in Tamil Nadu, it was customary for the eldest son to succeed the father in the royal family, and for the king and ministers to rule the country together. In these days, it is desired that monarchies should not exist, and that citizens should govern themselves. For this, a democratic system of government, with many capable leaders holding royal positions, is sought. These ideas are all products of new civilization. However, in our ancient times, the policy of righteous people was that the world should be governed by a king. Here, we can see our author Sekkizhar Peruman, a great minister, conveying this truth through the words of the ministers for the salvation of the world. Sivakavimani Ayya comments that this path is divinely supported, which is also derived from this history. A country must be sustained by a king. That king must rule by considering the six elements of the state: army, populace, resources, ministry, allies, and fortifications. A country without a king to protect all living beings and the world, allowing them to live according to their respective paths under his great umbrella, is like a body without a soul. A soulless body is an object that can be placed as others wish. Similarly, a country without a king will be an object manipulated by others not belonging to that country. "Murai seythu kaappaatrum mannavan makkal irai endru vaikkappadum" (The king who rules justly and protects his people is considered their god) is from Thirukkural. "Nellum uyir andre neerum uyir andre mannar uyirthe malarthalai ulagam" (Neither paddy nor water is life; the king is the life of this wide world) is from Purananuru. The body cannot live without the soul. Similarly, this earth cannot live without a king. Its meaning is that it will be thrown into disarray and perish. Considering all these things, the ministers all gather and make a decision: "Now there is no king here. This is what must be done." We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 10 — Murthi Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 189, Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Murthi Nayanar Puranam. The ministers gather and make a decision to choose the next king. They decide to blindfold an elephant and send it on a procession through the city, and whoever the elephant carries will be the king. Sekkizhar Peruman sings: "They worshipped God with rituals of perfect glory as prescribed in the scriptures, adorned the elephant with a garland on its forehead, blindfolded its eyes with fragrant cloth, and sent it forth, saying, 'Bring forth a noble one to bear the kingship of this great land with his mighty shoulders.'" Here, "nool murai" (scriptural method) referred to by Sekkizhar Swamigal means "as prescribed by Agamic texts." The phrase "nool murai semmaan vinai archanai seythu" appears in this verse. The three adjectives "semmai" (perfection), "maanbu" (glory), and "vinai" (action/ritual) are used here as adjectives for "archanai" (worship). Sekkizhar Swamigal has used these three adjectives for "archanai" to indicate that the worship was performed perfectly, with all three components of worship – bhavana (meditation), mantra (incantation), and kriya (ritual) – properly integrated. This is because, as a result of this worship performed according to the Shiva Agamic texts, they are going to receive a king who will rule the country with Shiva symbols, ensuring that the tenets of Saivism flourish everywhere. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal has sung with such emphasis. In ancient times in our country, it was customary for noble people to worship God as prescribed in Agamic texts, blindfold an elephant, and accept the person carried by that elephant as king. This is also a practice prescribed by scriptures, as indicated by "nool murai." Why should the elephant's eyes be blindfolded? So that the elephant does not choose a person based on its past familiarity or other factors, with enmity or affection. Therefore, its eyes are blindfolded. Thus, it is customary that if God is worshipped as prescribed in the Agamas, the elephant's rituals are performed, and its eyes are blindfolded, then the elephant, guided by the divine within, will carry a suitable person. It is historical fact that Chola Karikalan Peruvallathan was brought by an elephant from Kazhumala and chosen to be king. This is found in many ancient Tamil texts. Regarding this, Sivakavimani Ayya provides some notes from Pandit Na.Mu. Venkatasami Nattar. It is as follows: Apart from the story of Karikalan, no other ancient history is known about choosing a king by elephant. The proverb "Kazhumalaththiyaaththa kalirum karuvoor vizhumiyon mer sendradhanaal vizhumiya vendinum vendaavidinum murarpaala theendaa viduthal arithu" (The elephant tied in Kazhumalam went towards the noble one in Karuvoor; even if one desires or not, it is difficult to avoid what is destined) and its commentary, which states that the elephant tied in Kazhumalam went towards Karikala Valavan, who was in Karuvoor, and though he was very young, because he was noble, it picked him up and made him eligible for kingship, are the basis for Karikalan's story. The Sevvanthi Puranam states that when Uraiyur was destroyed by a sandstorm, the ministers sent an elephant to bring Karikalan, the son of Parantakan. Even when Pallava kings and others were emperors in Tamil Nadu, their right to be crowned is not evident. The Periya Puranam preserves the tradition that only the lineage of the three crowned kings (Chera, Chola, Pandya) had the right to be crowned. When Kootruva Nayanar defeated other kings and became king himself, the Thillai Vaazh Andhanars (Brahmins of Chidambaram) refused to crown him. Therefore, he sought and received Lord Shiva's feet as his crown, as mentioned in the Periya Puranam. Thus, Murthi Nayanar and Kootruva Nayanar accepted kingship and ruled the country. The fact that they, unlike other kings, took matted hair and the Lord's feet as their crowns, clearly shows that the Periya Puranam is structured in such a way that the tradition that only the three crowned kings have the right to wear a golden crown is not violated. Madhurapuri, also known as Madurai, is an ancient city with defensive fortifications. As Appar Swamigal sang in his Kappu Thiruththandaagam, "Kannudalaan thannudaiya kaappukkale" (It is the protection of the one with the eye on the forehead), Madurai is a city protected by the Lord. In such streets, the blindfolded elephant proceeds through many streets. Knowing all this, the elephant is now approaching the Thiru Aalavaai temple, surrounded by the great Kapali fort. The Madurai temple is also called Thiru Aalavaai, and its fort is called Kapali fort. "Kapali neel kadi madhil koodal Aalavaayilaay" is from Thirugnanasambandha Peruman's Thevaram. Murthi Nayanar, inside the temple, learns of all the events that transpired during the night. At that moment, he emerges from the Thiru Aalavaai temple. The divine utterance previously spoken by Lord Shiva also comes to his mind. Lord Shiva had blessed him, saying, "This king will die. Therefore, you yourself rule this world, perform your sacred service, and then come to our Shiva Lokam." That too comes to Murthi Nayanar's mind. Now, the royal elephant is wandering through the streets of Madurai and approaching the fort wall of the Thiru Aalavaai temple. Therefore, Murthi Nayanar, thinking, "If this is Lord Shiva's will, so be it," comes out of the temple and stands there. This world had performed penance.
